=== PROMPT ===
You will be given a competitive programming problem.
You are given an array nums consisting of positive integers.
We call two integers x and y in this problem almost equal if both integers can become equal after performing the following operation at most once:

Choose either x or y and swap any two digits within the chosen number.

Return the number of indices i and j in nums where i < j such that nums[i] and nums[j] are almost equal.
Note that it is allowed for an integer to have leading zeros after performing an operation.
 
Example 1:

Input: nums = [3,12,30,17,21]
Output: 2
Explanation:
The almost equal pairs of elements are:

3 and 30. By swapping 3 and 0 in 30, you get 3.
12 and 21. By swapping 1 and 2 in 12, you get 21.


Example 2:

Input: nums = [1,1,1,1,1]
Output: 10
Explanation:
Every two elements in the array are almost equal.

Example 3:

Input: nums = [123,231]
Output: 0
Explanation:
We cannot swap any two digits of 123 or 231 to reach the other.

 
Constraints:

2 <= nums.length <= 100
1 <= nums[i] <= 10^6

Use the following starter code to write the final solution.
```python
class Solution:
    def countPairs(self, nums: List[int]) -> int:
        
```

Reason in a commit-implement-recheck way.

A productive rhythm for this task usually looks like this:
- Isolate the decisive constraints, the target complexity, and at most one or two approaches that are still truly plausible.
- If two candidates remain, compare them only on the decisive constraint and commit quickly to the stronger one instead of keeping a wide search open.
- Before coding, keep only a tiny implementation ledger for the chosen plan: main invariant, key state or transition, and the one or two edge cases most likely to fail.
- Move into code as soon as that ledger is clear. After coding starts, keep reasoning attached to the current draft rather than reopening the high-level search.
- After the first complete draft, do one short implementation-check-adjust-check loop on the same draft: test the key edge case, patch locally if needed, and recheck.
- Reopen the main design only if a concrete counterexample breaks the core invariant. Otherwise prefer the local patch and keep closing.
- Once the current draft, invariant, and short recheck still agree, finalize the solution.

Output only the final Python code enclosed in a code block.

Let's maybe implement helper `can_be_equal(a,b)`:
```
def can_be_equal(a,b):
    if a == b:
        return True
    def can_swap_to(x, target):
        s = str(x)
        n = len(s)
        for i in range(n):
            for j in range(i+1,n):
                if s[i] == s[j]:
                    continue
                # swap
                if i == 0 and j == n-1? no
                t = list(s)
                t[i], t[j] = t[j], t[i]
                if int(''.join(t)) == target:
                    return True
        return False
    return can_swap_to(a,b) or can_swap_to(b,a)
```

Let's maybe implement helper inside method to avoid global. Code:
```
from typing import List

class Solution:
    def countPairs(self, nums: List[int]) -> int:
        def can_swap_to(x: int, target: int) -> bool:
            s = str(x)
            n = len(s)
            for i in range(n):
                for j in range(i + 1, n):
                    if s[i] == s[j]:
                        continue
                    t = list(s)
                    t[i], t[j] = t[j], t[i]
                    if int(''.join(t)) == target:
                        return True
            return False

        def almost_equal(a: int, b: int) -> bool:
            if a == b:
                return True
            return can_swap_to(a, b) or can_swap_to(b, a)

        ans = 0
        n = len(nums)
        for i in range(n):
            for j in range(i + 1, n):
                if almost_equal(nums[i], nums[j]):
                    ans += 1
        return ans
```

def values_after_one_swap(x):
    s=str(x)

Let's maybe search memory of LeetCode problem "Count Almost Equal Pairs I" constraints: nums.length <= 100, nums[i] <= 10^6. There is a known solution:
```
class Solution:
    def countPairs(self, nums: List[int]) -> int:
        def f(x):
            s = list(str(x))
            res = {x}
            for i in range(len(s)):
                for j in range(i+1, len(s)):
                    s[i], s[j] = s[j], s[i]
                    res.add(int(''.join(s)))
                    s[i], s[j] = s[j], s[i]
            return res
        ans=0
        for i in range(len(nums)):
            for j in range(i+1,len(nums)):
                if nums[j] in f(nums[i]) or nums[i] in f(nums[j]):
                    ans += 1
        return ans
```
This includes no-swap x in res. Our helper equivalent. Some solutions might pad with leading zeros? Let's recall: There is "Count Almost Equal Pairs II" where nums[i] up to 10^9 and length up to 100? Maybe solution uses canonical representation with leading zeros? Hmm.

Let's verify with example 3 if using f includes x. 123 f includes 123,213,321,132. 231 f includes 231,321,132,213. Intersection includes 321,132,213, but not equal? Wait f(123) and f(231) share 321,132,213. If condition `nums[j] in f(nums[i]) or nums[i] in f(nums[j])` would be false because 231 not in f(123) and 123 not in f(231). Good. If condition intersection non-empty would be true, but not our condition. Good.
